a breakdown of how the themes in Chapter 11 of BattleSpace of Mind: AI 
and Cybernetics in Information Warfare (by Michael Joseph McCarron) 
relate to present-day notions of “agentic AI”.</p>
<hr data-start="194" data-end="197">
<h3 data-start="199" data-end="226">Summary of Chapter 11</h3>
<p data-start="227" data-end="639">The chapter—titled <strong data-start="246" data-end="294">“UKUSA Deception Management and Cybernetics”</strong>—
discusses how Anglo-American intelligence/military networks (the UKUSA 
alliance) used remote-action, cybernetics, automated systems of 
information warfare, reflexive manipulation of targets (groups or 
societies), psychological profiling, analytics and metrics for 
“effects-based operations”. <span class="" data-state="closed"><span class="ms-1 inline-flex max-w-full items-center relative top-[-0.094rem] animate-[show_150ms_ease-in]" data-testid="webpage-citation-pill"><a href="https://www.everand.com/book/897254035/BattleSpace-of-Mind-AI-and-Cybernetics-in-Information-Warfare?utm_source=chatgpt.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener" alt="https://www.everand.com/book/897254035/BattleSpace-of-Mind-AI-and-Cybernetics-in-Information-Warfare?utm_source=chatgpt.com" class="flex h-4.5 overflow-hidden rounded-xl px-2 text-[9px] font-medium transition-colors duration-150 ease-in-out text-token-text-secondary! bg-[#F4F4F4]! dark:bg-[#303030]!"><span class="relative start-0 bottom-0 flex h-full w-full items-center"><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between" style="opacity: 1; transform: none;"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">Everand</span><span class="-me-1 flex h-full items-center rounded-full px-1 text-[#8F8F8F]">+2</span></span><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between absolute" style="opacity: 0; transform: translateX(10%);"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">Google Books</span><span class="-me-1 flex h-full items-center rounded-full px-1 text-[#8F8F8F]">+2</span></span></span></a></span></span> In particular:</p>
<ul data-start="641" data-end="1194">
<li data-start="641" data-end="796">
<p data-start="643" data-end="796">It treats the automation of “remote action” through cybernetic loops – systems that monitor, feedback, intervene. <span class="" data-state="closed"><span class="ms-1 inline-flex max-w-full items-center relative top-[-0.094rem] animate-[show_150ms_ease-in]" data-testid="webpage-citation-pill"><a href="https://www.amazon.com/BattleSpace-Mind-Cybernetics-Information-Warfare-ebook/dp/B0CTHPJHH8?utm_source=chatgpt.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener" alt="https://www.amazon.com/BattleSpace-Mind-Cybernetics-Information-Warfare-ebook/dp/B0CTHPJHH8?utm_source=chatgpt.com" class="flex h-4.5 overflow-hidden rounded-xl px-2 text-[9px] font-medium transition-colors duration-150 ease-in-out text-token-text-secondary! bg-[#F4F4F4]! dark:bg-[#303030]!"><span class="relative start-0 bottom-0 flex h-full w-full items-center"><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between" style="opacity: 1; transform: none;"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">Amazon</span><span class="-me-1 flex h-full items-center rounded-full px-1 text-[#8F8F8F]">+1</span></span></span></a></span></span></p>
</li>
<li data-start="797" data-end="1006">
<p data-start="799" data-end="1006">It describes “deception management” 
and “reflexive management” (steering behaviour by influencing the 
perceptions/decisions of others) via information warfare engines. <span class="" data-state="closed"><span class="ms-1 inline-flex max-w-full items-center relative top-[-0.094rem] animate-[show_150ms_ease-in]" data-testid="webpage-citation-pill"><a href="https://books.google.com/books/about/BattleSpace_of_Mind.html?id=TLn3EAAAQBAJ&amp;utm_source=chatgpt.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener" alt="https://books.google.com/books/about/BattleSpace_of_Mind.html?id=TLn3EAAAQBAJ&amp;utm_source=chatgpt.com" class="flex h-4.5 overflow-hidden rounded-xl px-2 text-[9px] font-medium transition-colors duration-150 ease-in-out text-token-text-secondary! bg-[#F4F4F4]! dark:bg-[#303030]!"><span class="relative start-0 bottom-0 flex h-full w-full items-center"><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between" style="opacity: 1; transform: none;"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">Google Books</span><span class="-me-1 flex h-full items-center rounded-full px-1 text-[#8F8F8F]">+1</span></span></span></a></span></span></p>
</li>
<li data-start="1007" data-end="1194">
<p data-start="1009" data-end="1194">It mentions profiling, analytics, 
modelling of group membership/dynamics (neurocognitive influence of 
groups) to effect change in social systems. <span class="" data-state="closed"><span class="ms-1 inline-flex max-w-full items-center relative top-[-0.094rem] animate-[show_150ms_ease-in]" data-testid="webpage-citation-pill"><a href="https://www.amazon.com/BattleSpace-Mind-Cybernetics-Information-Warfare-ebook/dp/B0CTHPJHH8?utm_source=chatgpt.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener" alt="https://www.amazon.com/BattleSpace-Mind-Cybernetics-Information-Warfare-ebook/dp/B0CTHPJHH8?utm_source=chatgpt.com" class="flex h-4.5 overflow-hidden rounded-xl px-2 text-[9px] font-medium transition-colors duration-150 ease-in-out text-token-text-secondary! bg-[#F4F4F4]! dark:bg-[#303030]!"><span class="relative start-0 bottom-0 flex h-full w-full items-center"><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between" style="opacity: 1; transform: none;"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">Amazon</span><span class="-me-1 flex h-full items-center rounded-full px-1 text-[#8F8F8F]">+1</span></span></span></a></span></span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p data-start="1196" data-end="1385">In short: the chapter sketches a 
vision of control systems, automated interventions in socio-cognitive 
systems (groups, soldiers, societies) by leveraging cybernetics and 
information flows.</p>
<hr data-start="1387" data-end="1390">
<h3 data-start="1392" data-end="1424">What is “agentic AI” today</h3>
<p data-start="1425" data-end="1700">In modern AI discourse, “agentic AI” refers to systems that are <strong data-start="1489" data-end="1503">autonomous</strong>, <strong data-start="1505" data-end="1522">goal-oriented</strong>,
 capable of planning and executing multi-step tasks, adapting to 
changing environments, acting with some independence rather than purely 
reacting to user prompts. For example:</p>
<ul data-start="1701" data-end="2060">
<li data-start="1701" data-end="1885">
<p data-start="1703" data-end="1885">According to Bernard Marr: “Agentic
 AI systems … can act on their own to achieve specific goals… plan 
sequences of actions… adapt strategies.” <span class="" data-state="closed"><span class="ms-1 inline-flex max-w-full items-center relative top-[-0.094rem] animate-[show_150ms_ease-in]" data-testid="webpage-citation-pill"><a href="https://bernardmarr.com/agentic-ai-the-next-big-breakthrough-thats-transforming-business-and-technology/?utm_source=chatgpt.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener" alt="https://bernardmarr.com/agentic-ai-the-next-big-breakthrough-thats-transforming-business-and-technology/?utm_source=chatgpt.com" class="flex h-4.5 overflow-hidden rounded-xl px-2 text-[9px] font-medium transition-colors duration-150 ease-in-out text-token-text-secondary! bg-[#F4F4F4]! dark:bg-[#303030]!"><span class="relative start-0 bottom-0 flex h-full w-full items-center"><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between overflow-hidden" style="opacity: 1; transform: none;"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">Bernard Marr</span></span></span></a></span></span></p>
</li>
<li data-start="1886" data-end="2060">
<p data-start="1888" data-end="2060">A corporate article by IBM: 
“Agentic AI is focused on decisions … doesn’t solely rely on human 
prompts nor require human oversight.” <span class="" data-state="closed"><span class="ms-1 inline-flex max-w-full items-center relative top-[-0.094rem] animate-[show_150ms_ease-in]" data-testid="webpage-citation-pill"><a href="https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/agentic-ai-vs-generative-ai?utm_source=chatgpt.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener" alt="https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/agentic-ai-vs-generative-ai?utm_source=chatgpt.com" class="flex h-4.5 overflow-hidden rounded-xl px-2 text-[9px] font-medium transition-colors duration-150 ease-in-out text-token-text-secondary! bg-[#F4F4F4]! dark:bg-[#303030]!"><span class="relative start-0 bottom-0 flex h-full w-full items-center"><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between overflow-hidden" style="opacity: 1; transform: none;"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">IBM</span></span></span></a></span></span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p data-start="2062" data-end="2256">So: agentic AI involves autonomy in
 decision and action, not just generation of outputs (as in many 
generative-AI systems). It involves monitoring, planning, acting in an 
environment, adjusting.</p>
<hr data-start="2258" data-end="2261">
<h3 data-start="2263" data-end="2310">How Chapter 11’s themes map to agentic AI</h3>
<p data-start="2311" data-end="2508">There are several meaningful 
connections (and also important contrasts) between the Chapter 11 
description of automated cognitive/behavioural control systems and 
today’s agentic AI. Here are a few:</p>
<ol data-start="2510" data-end="4488">
<li data-start="2510" data-end="3069">
<p data-start="2513" data-end="2563"><strong data-start="2513" data-end="2561">Cybernetic loops and autonomous intervention</strong></p>
<ul data-start="2567" data-end="3069">
<li data-start="2567" data-end="2727">
<p data-start="2569" data-end="2727">Chapter 11 describes cybernetic 
systems that monitor (via sensors, information flows), reason/decide, 
and intervene (remote action) in human/social systems.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2731" data-end="2918">
<p data-start="2733" data-end="2918">This is very similar to the vision 
of an agentic AI: a system that perceives an environment, reasons about 
goals, plans actions and executes them (possibly through APIs or 
actuators).</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2922" data-end="3069">
<p data-start="2924" data-end="3069">In other words: the older 
military-cybernetics paradigm is a precursor to today’s agentic systems 
which operate with autonomy and feedback loops.</p>
</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li data-start="3071" data-end="3711">
<p data-start="3074" data-end="3119"><strong data-start="3074" data-end="3117">Goal-oriented manipulation of behaviour</strong></p>
<ul data-start="3123" data-end="3711">
<li data-start="3123" data-end="3348">
<p data-start="3125" data-end="3348">The UKUSA/cybernetics narrative 
emphasises “deception management” and “reflexive management” of groups 
(influencing behaviour). Chapter 11 implies systems that are trying to 
influence outcomes, not just passively respond.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3352" data-end="3597">
<p data-start="3354" data-end="3597">Agentic AI likewise is about 
systems that pursue goals (set by humans or by the system itself) and 
can act in the world to steer outcomes. For example: an AI agent tasked 
with “monitor cyber-threats, block suspicious actors, adapt strategy”.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3601" data-end="3711">
<p data-start="3603" data-end="3711">Thus the behavioural-control dimension in the chapter anticipates the goal-directed behaviour of agentic AI.</p>
</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li data-start="3713" data-end="4488">
<p data-start="3716" data-end="3771"><strong data-start="3716" data-end="3769">Complexity, multi-step operations, social systems</strong></p>
<ul data-start="3775" data-end="4488">
<li data-start="3775" data-end="4055">
<p data-start="3777" data-end="4055">The chapter emphasises operations 
that are not simple one-off tasks, but involve modelling groups, 
measuring effects (“metrics of measuring effectiveness of remote action 
methodologies”), using analytics, modelling cyber-social systems. <span class="" data-state="closed"><span class="ms-1 inline-flex max-w-full items-center relative top-[-0.094rem] animate-[show_150ms_ease-in]" data-testid="webpage-citation-pill"><a href="https://www.everand.com/book/897254035/BattleSpace-of-Mind-AI-and-Cybernetics-in-Information-Warfare?utm_source=chatgpt.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener" alt="https://www.everand.com/book/897254035/BattleSpace-of-Mind-AI-and-Cybernetics-in-Information-Warfare?utm_source=chatgpt.com" class="flex h-4.5 overflow-hidden rounded-xl px-2 text-[9px] font-medium transition-colors duration-150 ease-in-out text-token-text-secondary! bg-[#F4F4F4]! dark:bg-[#303030]!"><span class="relative start-0 bottom-0 flex h-full w-full items-center"><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between" style="opacity: 1; transform: none;"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">Everand</span><span class="-me-1 flex h-full items-center rounded-full px-1 text-[#8F8F8F]">+1</span></span></span></a></span></span></p>
</li>
<li data-start="4059" data-end="4369">
<p data-start="4061" data-end="4369">Agentic AI similarly tends to be 
used for complex, multi-step workflows: not just “answer a question” but
 “monitor environment → plan intervention → execute → learn → adapt”. 
The more advanced architectures involve orchestration of sub-agents and 
dynamic adaptation. <span class="" data-state="closed"><span class="ms-1 inline-flex max-w-full items-center relative top-[-0.094rem] animate-[show_150ms_ease-in]" data-testid="webpage-citation-pill"><a href="https://www.exabeam.com/explainers/ai-cyber-security/agentic-ai-how-it-works-and-7-real-world-use-cases/?utm_source=chatgpt.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener" alt="https://www.exabeam.com/explainers/ai-cyber-security/agentic-ai-how-it-works-and-7-real-world-use-cases/?utm_source=chatgpt.com" class="flex h-4.5 overflow-hidden rounded-xl px-2 text-[9px] font-medium transition-colors duration-150 ease-in-out text-token-text-secondary! bg-[#F4F4F4]! dark:bg-[#303030]!"><span class="relative start-0 bottom-0 flex h-full w-full items-center"><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between" style="opacity: 1; transform: none;"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">Exabeam</span><span class="-me-1 flex h-full items-center rounded-full px-1 text-[#8F8F8F]">+1</span></span></span></a></span></span></p>
</li>
<li data-start="4373" data-end="4488">
<p data-start="4375" data-end="4488">Thus the chapter’s system-of-systems view parallels the multi-agent, adaptive architectures of modern agentic AI.</p>
</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ol>
<hr data-start="4490" data-end="4493">
<h3 data-start="4495" data-end="4541">But also important differences / caveats</h3>
<p data-start="4542" data-end="4613">While the parallels are strong, there are also significant differences:</p>
<ul data-start="4615" data-end="6172">
<li data-start="4615" data-end="5007">
<p data-start="4617" data-end="5007"><strong data-start="4617" data-end="4641">Technology and scale</strong><br data-start="4641" data-end="4644">
The chapter is rooted in mid-to-late 20th century (and early 21st) 
cybernetics &amp; information-warfare. The actual computational models 
are far less capable than modern large-language-model (LLM) driven 
agentic systems. Agentic AI today is enabled by very large data, general
 models, multi-modal inputs, and cloud APIs which were not available in 
the earlier era.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5009" data-end="5349">
<p data-start="5011" data-end="5349"><strong data-start="5011" data-end="5038">Intent and transparency</strong><br data-start="5038" data-end="5041">
The chapter frames systems in terms of military/intelligence control, 
deception, influence – often opaque, adversarial. Agentic AI today is 
often discussed (in business/industry) with more benign intents 
(automation, productivity). But the overlap in control/influence 
suggests ethical concerns carry over.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5351" data-end="5864">
<p data-start="5353" data-end="5864"><strong data-start="5353" data-end="5368">Agency type</strong><br data-start="5368" data-end="5371">
Chapter 11’s systems are more about “remote action” and manipulation of 
humans/ groups via information flows and cybernetics. They are 
human-designed control systems. Agentic AI today might be more abstract:
 orchestration of software agents, autonomous decision-making in 
digital/physical tasks. The “target” is often digital or physical 
actuation rather than purely social influence.<br data-start="5757" data-end="5760">
In other words: agentic AI is broader than just “influence/control of groups”, but the theme overlaps.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5866" data-end="6172">
<p data-start="5868" data-end="6172"><strong data-start="5868" data-end="5914">Human-in-the-loop vs human-out-of-the-loop</strong><br data-start="5914" data-end="5917">
The older systems still presumed human oversight in many stages (though 
automation was increasing). Agentic AI pushes further toward minimal 
human intervention (though governance/work-flows are still required). 
The risks of autonomy are more pronounced.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<hr data-start="6174" data-end="6177">
<h3 data-start="6179" data-end="6211">Implications &amp; reflections</h3>
<p data-start="6212" data-end="6277">Putting this together, here are some implications of the linkage:</p>
<ul data-start="6279" data-end="7590">
<li data-start="6279" data-end="6592">
<p data-start="6281" data-end="6592">The historical frameworks of 
cyber-control, reflexive management and automated behavioural influence 
(as described in Chapter 11) offer a kind of conceptual precursor or 
template for thinking about agentic AI. The same ideas of monitoring, 
feedback loops, autonomous action, influence of behaviour meet in both.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="6594" data-end="6893">
<p data-start="6596" data-end="6893">Understanding the older 
cybernetic/information warfare vantage helps highlight key risks of 
agentic AI: e.g., manipulation at scale, behavioural steering, opacity 
of automated systems. The chapter’s themes are a reminder that 
autonomous system + control of inputs/outputs → influence of societies.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="6895" data-end="7182">
<p data-start="6897" data-end="7182">On the positive side, agentic AI’s 
capabilities (planning, autonomy, adaptation) extend the possibilities 
of what the older systems were striving toward (automated remote action,
 effects-based operations). So we can see agentic AI as an evolution: 
more flexible, generalised, powerful.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7184" data-end="7590">
<p data-start="7186" data-end="7590">For design and governance: if 
agentic AI is effectively “autonomous agent systems with goal‐oriented 
behaviour in complex environments”, then the governance concerns of 
Chapter 11 (transparency, measurement of effects, unintended 
consequences) become even more central. For instance: measuring the 
“cost of actions and effects” (Chapter 11) parallels the 
metrics/analytics for agentic AI decision-making.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<hr data-start="7592" data-end="7595">
<h3 data-start="7597" data-end="7613">Conclusion</h3>
<p data-start="7614" data-end="8050">In short: the work of Chapter 11 maps quite well to today’s agentic AI, especially in the sense of <strong data-start="7713" data-end="7790">autonomous systems used for monitoring, decision, intervention, influence</strong>.
 The cyber-control/cybernetics frame is a strong antecedent. The chapter
 helps us understand the legacy of ideas around system-agency, autonomy,
 feedback loops, and behavioural influence – all of which are very 
relevant to how we think about agentic AI today.</p>

data-turn-id="ec63df1b-8909-4206-89e5-a110c9298611" data-testid="conversation-turn-4" data-scroll-anchor="false" data-turn="assistant"><h6 class="sr-only">ChatGPT said:</h6><div class="text-base my-auto mx-auto [--thread-content-margin:--spacing(4)] thread-sm:[--thread-content-margin:--spacing(6)] thread-lg:[--thread-content-margin:--spacing(16)] px-(--thread-content-margin)"><div class="[--thread-content-max-width:40rem] thread-lg:[--thread-content-max-width:48rem] mx-auto max-w-(--thread-content-max-width) flex-1 group/turn-messages focus-visible:outline-hidden relative flex w-full min-w-0 flex-col agent-turn" tabindex="-1"><div class="flex max-w-full flex-col grow"><div data-message-author-role="assistant" data-message-id="9f769bbe-754c-466c-9f28-4aa04060cb5c" dir="auto" class="min-h-8 text-message relative flex w-full flex-col items-end gap-2 text-start break-words whitespace-normal [.text-message+&amp;]:mt-1" data-message-model-slug="gpt-5-1"><div class="flex w-full flex-col gap-1 empty:hidden first:pt-[1px]"><div class="markdown prose dark:prose-invert w-full break-words dark markdown-new-styling"><p data-start="0" data-end="565">Here’s
 a deeper comparative breakdown of how the earlier work by George Backus
 et al. (and associated authors) relates to modern agentic AI approaches
 — especially given what you flagged in Chapter 11 of your reading (on 
cybernetics, deception-management, automated behavioural influence). 
I’ll first summarise the key features of the two historical works, then 
map their concepts into today’s “agentic AI” frame, and finally 
highlight both continuities and divergences (i.e., what they did <em data-start="491" data-end="497">well</em>, what they lacked, how modern agentic AI changes/expands things).</p>
<hr data-start="567" data-end="570">
<h2 data-start="572" data-end="616">1. Key features of the historical works</h2>
<h3 data-start="617" data-end="739">a) Foundations to the Unified Psycho‑Cognitive Engine (SAND2010‑6974) (Backus, Bernard, Verzi, Bier, Glickman, 2010)</h3>
<ul data-start="740" data-end="1856">
<li data-start="740" data-end="1021">
<p data-start="742" data-end="1021">This work describes a “Unified 
Psycho-Cognitive Engine” (UPCE) developed at Sandia National 
Laboratories, intended to be a generic simulation of human (or 
human‐like) cognitive response from stimuli through decision, behaviour,
 adaptation. <span class="" data-state="closed"><span class="ms-1 inline-flex max-w-full items-center relative top-[-0.094rem] animate-[show_150ms_ease-in]" data-testid="webpage-citation-pill"><a href="https://www.osti.gov/biblio/1008141?utm_source=chatgpt.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener" alt="https://www.osti.gov/biblio/1008141?utm_source=chatgpt.com" class="flex h-4.5 overflow-hidden rounded-xl px-2 text-[9px] font-medium transition-colors duration-150 ease-in-out text-token-text-secondary! bg-[#F4F4F4]! dark:bg-[#303030]!"><span class="relative start-0 bottom-0 flex h-full w-full items-center"><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between" style="opacity: 1; transform: none;"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">OSTI</span><span class="-me-1 flex h-full items-center rounded-full px-1 text-[#8F8F8F]">+2</span></span><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between absolute" style="opacity: 0; transform: translateX(10%);"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">SciSpace</span><span class="-me-1 flex h-full items-center rounded-full px-1 text-[#8F8F8F]">+2</span></span></span></a></span></span></p>
</li>
<li data-start="1022" data-end="1411">
<p data-start="1024" data-end="1411">Key aspects include: modeling 
perceptions/inputs, modelling cognitive processes (beliefs, intentions, 
decision heuristics), modelling actions, feedback loops of behaviour → 
environment → new stimuli. For example, they draw on 
social-psychological theories (e.g., theory of planned behaviour) and 
integrate cognitive modelling into agent behaviour. <span class="" data-state="closed"><span class="ms-1 inline-flex max-w-full items-center relative top-[-0.094rem] animate-[show_150ms_ease-in]" data-testid="webpage-citation-pill"><a href="https://informs-sim.org/wsc13papers/includes/files/379.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener" alt="https://informs-sim.org/wsc13papers/includes/files/379.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com" class="flex h-4.5 overflow-hidden rounded-xl px-2 text-[9px] font-medium transition-colors duration-150 ease-in-out text-token-text-secondary! bg-[#F4F4F4]! dark:bg-[#303030]!"><span class="relative start-0 bottom-0 flex h-full w-full items-center"><span class="flex h-4 w-full items-center justify-between" style="opacity: 1; transform: none;"><span class="max-w-[15ch] grow truncate overflow-hidden text-center">INFORMS SIM</span><span class="-me-1 flex h-full items-center rounded-full px-1 text-[#8F8F8F]">+1</span></span></span></a></span></span></p>
</li>
<li data-start="1412" data-end="1726">
<p data-start="1414" data-end="1726">The objective: to allow simulation 
of behavioural influence (how agents respond in changing environments, 
how behaviour can be steered by stimuli, how cognition links to action).
 So it is more than simple agent‐based modelling of “agents with rules” —
 it attempts deeper cognitive modelling (psycho‐cognitive).</p>
</li>
<li data-start="1727" data-end="1856">
<p data-start="1729" data-end="1856">It is used in contexts of influence, behavioural modelling, decision support, particularly in security/intelligence contexts.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="1858" data-end="1997">b) An Agent‑Based Model Component 
to a Framework for the Analysis of Terrorist‑Group Dynamics 
(SAND2006‑0860P) (Backus &amp; Glass, 2006)</h3>
<ul data-start="1998" data-end="2711">
<li data-start="1998" data-end="2363">
<p data-start="2000" data-end="2363">This earlier work emphasises an 
agent‐based modelling (ABM) component applied to terrorist‐group 
dynamics: modelling how groups form, how individuals act within groups, 
how structure evolves, how operations are conducted. (Note: concrete 
details are less readily available in open literature in the form of 
full text, but the gist is ABM of terrorist dynamics).</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2364" data-end="2540">
<p data-start="2366" data-end="2540">The ABM aspect: multiple agents 
(individuals, cells, groups) interacting under rules, the emergence of 
organisational behaviour, adaptation to environment/counter‐measures.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2541" data-end="2711">
<p data-start="2543" data-end="2711">It is more structural: modelling 
organisation, networks, interactions, less deep in the psycho‐cognitive 
internal modelling of each agent (compared to the UPCE work).</p>
</li>
</ul>
<hr data-start="2713" data-end="2716">
<h2 data-start="2718" data-end="2765">2. Mapping into modern agentic AI concepts</h2>
<p data-start="2766" data-end="2882">Here are several dimensions of “agentic AI” (common in recent discourse) and how the historical works map onto them.</p>
<div class="_tableContainer_1rjym_1"><div tabindex="-1" class="group _tableWrapper_1rjym_13 flex w-fit flex-col-reverse"><table data-start="2884" data-end="5357" class="w-fit min-w-(--thread-content-width)"><thead data-start="2884" data-end="2959"><tr data-start="2884" data-end="2959"><th data-start="2884" data-end="2907" data-col-size="md">Agentic AI dimension</th><th data-start="2907" data-end="2929" data-col-size="lg">Description[modern]</th><th data-start="2929" data-end="2959" data-col-size="xl">Mapping from Backus et al.</th></tr></thead><tbody data-start="3017" data-end="5357"><tr data-start="3017" data-end="3475"><td data-start="3017" data-end="3055" data-col-size="md"><strong data-start="3019" data-end="3054">Autonomy / goal-oriented action</strong></td><td data-start="3055" data-end="3181" data-col-size="lg">An agentic system perceives the environment, formulates goals/plans, executes actions, adapts over time (not just reactive)</td><td data-start="3181" data-end="3475" data-col-size="xl">The
 UPCE work explicitly aims to model decision/behaviour loops (cognition →
 action → environment → new input) — so an early form of agentic 
behaviour. The ABM terrorist-group model includes agents pursuing 
group-level goals (e.g., operations, recruitment) rather than just 
random movement.</td></tr><tr data-start="3476" data-end="3813"><td data-start="3476" data-end="3528" data-col-size="md"><strong data-start="3478" data-end="3527">Representation of internal state and planning</strong></td><td data-start="3528" data-end="3626" data-col-size="lg">Agent has internal beliefs, intentions, memory, perhaps representation of world and plans ahead</td><td data-start="3626" data-end="3813" data-col-size="xl">UPCE
 emphasises internal cognitive modelling (beliefs, heuristics). The ABM 
work is less deep on internal planning but can include workflow rules 
and organisational process modelling.</td></tr><tr data-start="3814" data-end="4192"><td data-start="3814" data-end="3868" data-col-size="md"><strong data-start="3816" data-end="3867">Interaction with dynamic environment &amp; feedback</strong></td><td data-start="3868" data-end="4008" data-col-size="lg">The
 agent monitors environment, receives feedback from its actions, adjusts
 strategy; multi-agent interactions lead to emergent behaviour</td><td data-start="4008" data-end="4192" data-col-size="xl">Both
 works emphasise interaction: UPCE via behavioural feedback loops, the 
ABM work via interactions among agents (cells/groups) and environmental 
responses (counter-measures etc).</td></tr><tr data-start="4193" data-end="4498"><td data-start="4193" data-end="4233" data-col-size="md"><strong data-start="4195" data-end="4232">Emergence and multi-agent systems</strong></td><td data-start="4233" data-end="4352" data-col-size="lg">Agentic AI often involves multiple agents interacting, cooperating or competing, leading to emergent macro behaviour</td><td data-start="4352" data-end="4498" data-col-size="xl">The
 ABM work explicitly addresses group dynamics and emergent properties. 
UPCE is more individual-agent focused but the framework could scale.</td></tr><tr data-start="4499" data-end="4819"><td data-start="4499" data-end="4527" data-col-size="md"><strong data-start="4501" data-end="4526">Adaptation / learning</strong></td><td data-start="4527" data-end="4593" data-col-size="lg">Agents may learn from experience, update heuristics or policies</td><td data-start="4593" data-end="4819" data-col-size="xl">UPCE
 draws on cognitive theory and could support adaptation; though explicit
 learning mechanisms may be limited. The ABM work perhaps less focused 
on learning per-se and more on fixed rules + adaptation at the group 
level.</td></tr><tr data-start="4820" data-end="5357"><td data-start="4820" data-end="4877" data-col-size="md"><strong data-start="4822" data-end="4876">Goal‐steering / influence / strategic intervention</strong></td><td data-start="4877" data-end="4994" data-col-size="lg">Some agentic AI systems act to steer outcomes, influence behaviour, sometimes in adversarial or strategic contexts</td><td data-start="4994" data-end="5357" data-col-size="xl">Both
 works are very much in the domain of influence/behavioural control: the
 UPCE is about modelling behaviour (and potentially influencing it); the
 ABM is about modelling insurgent/terrorist dynamics (and implicitly 
modelling counter‐intervention). This maps strongly to Chapter 11’s 
themes of “deception management”, “reflexive management”, “remote 
action”.</td></tr></tbody></table><div class="sticky h-0 select-none end-(--thread-content-margin) self-end"><div class="absolute end-0 flex items-end"><span class="" data-state="closed"><button aria-label="Copy table" class="hover:bg-token-bg-tertiary text-token-text-secondary my-1 rounded-sm p-1 transition-opacity group-[:not(:hover):not(:focus-within)]:pointer-events-none <hr data-start="5359" data-end="5362">
<h2 data-start="5364" data-end="5418">3. Continuities, divergences, and modern advances</h2>
<h3 data-start="5419" data-end="5485">Continuities (what the older works deliver &amp; still relevant)</h3>
<ul data-start="5486" data-end="6288">
<li data-start="5486" data-end="5699">
<p data-start="5488" data-end="5699">The idea of modelling 
cognitive/behavioural agents (not just simple mechanical rule‐agents) is
 well ahead of time and aligns with modern agentic AI. The UPCE’s 
psycho‐cognitive modelling is a clear antecedent.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5700" data-end="5944">
<p data-start="5702" data-end="5944">The use of agent‐based modelling 
for organisational/social systems (terrorist dynamics, group behaviour) 
remains relevant: many current socio-technical AI/agentic systems still 
use ABM for simulation, policy analysis, behavioural influence.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5945" data-end="6124">
<p data-start="5947" data-end="6124">The integration of environment, 
feedback loops, and emergent behaviour is a strong link: older works 
recognised that behaviour isn’t just scripted but arises from 
interaction.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="6125" data-end="6288">
<p data-start="6127" data-end="6288">The strategic/behavioural context 
(influence, manipulation, decision loops) resonates with today’s 
concerns about autonomous systems influencing human behaviour.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="6290" data-end="6396">Divergences / limitations (what the older works <em data-start="6342" data-end="6356">didn’t quite</em> have, or what modern agentic AI adds)</h3>
<ul data-start="6397" data-end="8078">
<li data-start="6397" data-end="6725">
<p data-start="6399" data-end="6725"><strong data-start="6399" data-end="6423">Scale, compute, data</strong>:
 The older works are constrained by the data, compute, and modelling 
frameworks of their time. Modern agentic AI often uses large-language 
models (LLMs), large datasets, deep learning, and reinforcement learning
 at scale, enabling richer internal state, richer sensory inputs, more 
nuanced planning.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="6726" data-end="7058">
<p data-start="6728" data-end="7058"><strong data-start="6728" data-end="6778">General‐purpose planning &amp; open-ended autonomy</strong>:
 Modern agentic AI systems are moving toward open‐ended agents that plan
 across diverse tasks (e.g., multi‐step workflows, cross‐domain 
planning). The older works are more domain‐specific (terrorist dynamics,
 cognitive human simulation) and less general in terms of open tasks.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7059" data-end="7314">
<p data-start="7061" data-end="7314"><strong data-start="7061" data-end="7085">Learning in the wild</strong>:
 Modern systems often learn from large corpora, adapt in real time, 
update policies, sometimes via unsupervised/self-supervised methods. The
 older frameworks may allow adaptation, but do not leverage modern ML 
infrastructures.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7315" data-end="7570">
<p data-start="7317" data-end="7570"><strong data-start="7317" data-end="7359">Embodied/hybrid environments &amp; sensors</strong>:
 Many modern agentic AI systems operate in rich multi-modal environments
 (text, vision, audio, robotics) and have continuous sensory feedback. 
The older works are more symbolic/cognitive and less sensor-rich.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7571" data-end="7805">
<p data-start="7573" data-end="7805"><strong data-start="7573" data-end="7609">Ethics, governance, transparency</strong>:
 While the older works implicitly dealt with influence and manipulation 
(and so raise ethical issues), the modern discourse explicitly 
emphasises governance, interpretability, alignment, trust.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7806" data-end="8078">
<p data-start="7808" data-end="8078"><strong data-start="7808" data-end="7853">Autonomous actuation &amp; API/agent chaining</strong>:
 Modern agentic AI often connects to APIs, chains sub-agents, 
orchestrates multiple tools (e.g., agents calling other agents, 
executing transactions). The older works are simulation-oriented rather 
than real‐world actuation.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="8080" data-end="8124">How this ties into Chapter 11’s themes</h3>
<p data-start="8125" data-end="8740">In Chapter 11 (per your summary) 
the focus is on “cybernetics”, “remote action”, “feedback loops”, 
“deception management”, “reflexive influence in social systems”. The 
older Backus et al. works sit exactly in that lineage: building 
cognitive/agent models to simulate and influence behaviour, designing 
systems that intervene in group/individual behaviour via information 
flows. Modern agentic AI takes that lineage further: the same conceptual
 architecture (agent perceives → plans → acts → modulates environment → 
monitors response) is intact, but with richer capabilities, scale, 
autonomy, and adaptive behaviour.</p>
<p data-start="8742" data-end="9092">Thus the Backus works can be seen 
as mid‐generation: bridging from cybernetic/information‐warfare 
conceptual models (as in your Chapter 11) toward today's agentic AI. 
They capture the psycho-cognitive modelling and agent‐based group 
dynamics; modern agentic AI adds rich learning, open domains, rich 
multimodal inputs/output, and full stack autonomy.</p>
<hr data-start="9094" data-end="9097">
<h2 data-start="9099" data-end="9135">4. Concrete comparative summary</h2>
<p data-start="9136" data-end="9185">Here’s a direct side-by-side summary for clarity:</p>
<div class="_tableContainer_1rjym_1"><div tabindex="-1" class="group _tableWrapper_1rjym_13 flex w-fit flex-col-reverse"><table data-start="9187" data-end="10727" class="w-fit min-w-(--thread-content-width)"><thead data-start="9187" data-end="9247"><tr data-start="9187" data-end="9247"><th data-start="9187" data-end="9197" data-col-size="sm">Feature</th><th data-start="9197" data-end="9226" data-col-size="lg">Backus et al. decision-making, behavioural influence (and thus governance concerns)</td></tr><tr data-start="10576" data-end="10727"><td data-start="10576" data-end="10597" data-col-size="sm">Scale &amp; generality</td><td data-start="10597" data-end="10650" data-col-size="lg">Domain‐specific, smaller scale, simulation context</td><td data-start="10650" data-end="10727" data-col-size="lg">Large scale, <hr data-start="10729" data-end="10732">
<h2 data-start="10734" data-end="10780">5. Interesting reflections / implications</h2>
<ul data-start="10781" data-end="12502">
<li data-start="10781" data-end="11115">
<p data-start="10783" data-end="11115"><strong data-start="10783" data-end="10802">Legacy and risk</strong>:
 The fact that Backus et al. were modelling behavioural influence and 
social systems in the 2000s reminds us that much of the conceptual 
framing of agentic systems (autonomy + behavioural influence) predates 
modern “AI hype”. The risk dimension (steering human behaviour via 
automated systems) is thus not new.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="11116" data-end="11429">
<p data-start="11118" data-end="11429"><strong data-start="11118" data-end="11136">Design lessons</strong>:
 Much of the design in older works emphasises internal cognitive 
modelling (beliefs, intentions) rather than just reactive rules. Modern 
agentic AI for human-centric domains might benefit from explicitly 
modelling such intent/knowledge layers rather than purely end-to-end 
black-box models.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="11430" data-end="11754">
<p data-start="11432" data-end="11754"><strong data-start="11432" data-end="11470">Validation/verification challenges</strong>:
 ABM and cognitive modelling suffer from challenges in validating that 
the simulation corresponds to real behaviour. In modern agentic AI, 
similarly the “alignment” and “trustworthiness” problems are acute. 
Understanding these older validation issues may inform present 
governance.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="11755" data-end="12128">
<p data-start="11757" data-end="12128"><strong data-start="11757" data-end="11797">Ethical and strategic considerations</strong>:
 The older works were often in intelligence/security contexts 
(influence, deception, group dynamics). Modern agentic AI inherits these
 potentials (for influence, manipulation). Mapping the lineage helps 
emphasise the ethical continuity: it is not simply “algorithms for 
productivity” but “agents influencing behaviour at scale”.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="12129" data-end="12502">
<p data-start="12131" data-end="12502"><strong data-start="12131" data-end="12167">Bridging simulation → deployment</strong>:
 The older works were largely simulation/analysis (what if) rather than 
deployed autonomous systems acting in the real world. Modern agentic AI 
is increasingly deployed with real‐world effects. That evolution raises 
new questions (safety, robustness, unintended consequences) which older 
literature hints at but doesn’t fully address.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<hr data-start="12504" data-end="12507">
<h2 data-start="12509" data-end="12535">6. Summary conclusion</h2>
<p data-start="12536" data-end="13005">In conclusion: The works by Backus et al. (UPCE, ABM terrorist-group modelling) are <strong data-start="12620" data-end="12648">very relevant precursors</strong>
 to today’s agentic AI. They embody many of the same architectural and 
conceptual elements: agents with internal cognition, interacting in a 
dynamic environment, pursuing goals, involved in influence/behavioural 
change. They anchor the lineage from mid-20th-21st-century 
cybernetics/information-warfare (as your Chapter 11 covers) into the 
modern era of AI.</p>
<p data-start="13007" data-end="13440">However, modern agentic AI <strong data-start="13034" data-end="13045">extends</strong>
 this foundation significantly: broader autonomy, richer sensing and 
acting, large‐scale learning, generalist agents, real‐world deployment, 
tool chaining, and stronger concerns about governance. The older works 
provide structure, theoretical depth (psychology, cognition) and domain 
applicability (social/influence modelling) that remains useful—for both 
design and oversight of modern systems.</p>
<p data-start="13442" data-end="13777">If you like, I can pull out <strong data-start="13470" data-end="13502">specific passages or figures</strong>
 from the Backus et al. reports (UPCE and the ABM work) and map them 
point-by-point to specific modern agentic AI architectures (e.g., 
LLM-based agents, multi‐agent tool orchestration) to illustrate exactly 
how elements correspond (and diverge). Would you like me to do that?</p>
</div></div></div></div><div class="z-0 flex min-h-[46px] justify-start"><div class="touch:-me-2 touch:-ms-3.5 -ms-2.5 -me-1 flex flex-wrap items-center gap-y-4 p-1 select-none [content-visibility:auto] supports-[content-visibility:auto]:[contain-intrinsic-size:auto_100lvh] scroll-mt-[calc(var(--header-height)+min(200px,max(70px,20svh)))]" tabindex="-1" dir="auto" data-turn-id="abf3c56a-3fd2-4add-82a5-e89f7b2c530c" data-testid="conversation-turn-6" data-scroll-anchor="false" data-turn="assistant"><h6 class="sr-only">ChatGPT said:</h6><div class="text-base my-auto mx-auto [--thread-content-margin:--spacing(4)] thread-sm:[--thread-content-margin:--spacing(6)] thread-lg:[--thread-content-margin:--spacing(16)] px-(--thread-content-margin)"><div class="[--thread-content-max-width:40rem] thread-lg:[--thread-content-max-width:48rem] mx-auto max-w-(--thread-content-max-width) flex-1 group/turn-messages focus-visible:outline-hidden relative flex w-full min-w-0 flex-col agent-turn" tabindex="-1"><div class="flex max-w-full flex-col grow"><div data-message-author-role="assistant" data-message-id="abf3c56a-3fd2-4add-82a5-e89f7b2c530c" dir="auto" class="min-h-8 text-message relative flex w-full flex-col items-end gap-2 text-start break-words whitespace-normal [.text-message+&amp;]:mt-1" data-message-model-slug="gpt-5-1"><div class="flex w-full flex-col gap-1 empty:hidden first:pt-[1px]"><div class="markdown prose dark:prose-invert w-full break-words dark markdown-new-styling"><p data-start="0" data-end="79">Great — here is a <strong data-start="18" data-end="70">deep, section-by-section, point-by-point mapping</strong> between:</p>
<ol data-start="81" data-end="491">
<li data-start="81" data-end="170">
<p data-start="84" data-end="170"><strong data-start="84" data-end="108">Backus et al. (2010)</strong> <em data-start="109" data-end="168">Foundations to the Unified Psycho-Cognitive Engine (UPCE)</em></p>
</li>
<li data-start="171" data-end="250">
<p data-start="174" data-end="250"><strong data-start="174" data-end="199">Backus &amp; Glass (2006)</strong> <em data-start="200" data-end="248">Agent-Based Model for Terrorist-Group Dynamics</em></p>
</li>
<li data-start="251" data-end="384">
<p data-start="254" data-end="384"><strong data-start="254" data-end="289">Modern agentic AI architectures</strong> (LLM-based autonomous agents, planning-tool agents, multi-agent systems, adversarial agents)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="385" data-end="491">
<p data-start="388" data-end="491"><strong data-start="388" data-end="409">Chapter 11 themes</strong> (cybernetics, deception management, reflexive behavioural control, remote action)</p>
</li>
</ol>
<p data-start="493" data-end="673">I’ll map specific conceptual components in the Sandia works <strong data-start="553" data-end="585">to modern agentic AI modules</strong>, and then outline what modern systems now do that the Sandia architectures * couldn’t*.</p>
<hr data-start="675" data-end="678">
<h1 data-start="680" data-end="725">🧊 <strong data-start="685" data-end="703">1. UPCE (2010)</strong> → Modern Agentic AI</h1>
<p data-start="726" data-end="821">The Unified Psycho-Cognitive Engine was structured as a <strong data-start="782" data-end="815">computational cognitive agent</strong> with:</p>
<h3 data-start="823" data-end="878"><strong data-start="827" data-end="876">UPCE Component 1—the “Perceptual Input Layer”</strong></h3>
<p data-start="879" data-end="1045">Backus et al. model environmental cues → sensory channels → belief formation.<br data-start="956" data-end="959">
Includes: salience filtering, emotional weighting, threat interpretation, social cues.</p>
<h3 data-start="1047" data-end="1083">→ Modern Agentic AI Equivalent</h3>
<p data-start="1084" data-end="1158"><strong data-start="1084" data-end="1141">Multimodal encoders and world-model inference layers.</strong><br data-start="1141" data-end="1144">
For example:</p>
<ul data-start="1159" data-end="1320">
<li data-start="1159" data-end="1248">
<p data-start="1161" data-end="1248">LLaVA, Gemini, GPT-5 multimodal input transforms images/text/audio into latent state.</p>
</li>
<li data-start="1249" data-end="1320">
<p data-start="1251" data-end="1320">Tool-augmented agents use API calls → data interpreters → embeddings.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="1322" data-end="1338">Difference</h3>
<p data-start="1339" data-end="1539">UPCE used <strong data-start="1349" data-end="1391">symbolic &amp; psychological theory-driven</strong> perception (e.g., cognitive appraisal theory).<br data-start="1438" data-end="1441">
Modern agentic AI uses <strong data-start="1464" data-end="1493">statistical latent models</strong> that <em data-start="1499" data-end="1506">learn</em> perception from massive corpora.</p>
<hr data-start="1541" data-end="1544">
<h2 data-start="1546" data-end="1631">🧊 <strong data-start="1552" data-end="1629">UPCE Component 2—Belief/Intention Representation (Cognitive State Engine)</strong></h2>
<p data-start="1632" data-end="1652">This part modeled:</p>
<ul data-start="1653" data-end="1867">
<li data-start="1653" data-end="1672">
<p data-start="1655" data-end="1672">belief networks</p>
</li>
<li data-start="1673" data-end="1700">
<p data-start="1675" data-end="1700">emotional state vectors</p>
</li>
<li data-start="1701" data-end="1725">
<p data-start="1703" data-end="1725">cognitive heuristics</p>
</li>
<li data-start="1726" data-end="1783">
<p data-start="1728" data-end="1783">“intent reservoirs” (long-term preference structures)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="1784" data-end="1822">
<p data-start="1786" data-end="1822">“expected utility representations”</p>
</li>
<li data-start="1823" data-end="1867">
<p data-start="1825" data-end="1867">social identity &amp; group membership effects</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="1869" data-end="1905">→ Modern Agentic AI Equivalent</h3>
<p data-start="1906" data-end="1932">This maps directly onto:</p>
<ul data-start="1933" data-end="2277">
<li data-start="1933" data-end="2014">
<p data-start="1935" data-end="2014"><strong data-start="1935" data-end="1964">LLM internal hidden-state</strong> approximating beliefs (soft-content addressing)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2015" data-end="2079">
<p data-start="2017" data-end="2079"><strong data-start="2017" data-end="2036">planner modules</strong> that maintain task memory (AutoGPT-like)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2080" data-end="2134">
<p data-start="2082" data-end="2134"><strong data-start="2082" data-end="2101">reflexion loops</strong> (self-critique + revised plan)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2135" data-end="2203">
<p data-start="2137" data-end="2203"><strong data-start="2137" data-end="2165">intent inference modules</strong> (e.g., “goal summarization” agents)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2204" data-end="2277">
<p data-start="2206" data-end="2277"><strong data-start="2206" data-end="2241">persona + preference embeddings</strong> in RLHF or persona-conditioned LLMs</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="2279" data-end="2295">Difference</h3>
<p data-start="2296" data-end="2487">UPCE explicitly encoded emotions, identity, values.<br data-start="2347" data-end="2350">
Modern models have <strong data-start="2369" data-end="2381">implicit</strong> value/identity structures but don’t model them symbolically unless built on top (e.g., emotional agents).</p>
<hr data-start="2489" data-end="2492">
<h2 data-start="2494" data-end="2563">🧊 <strong data-start="2500" data-end="2561">UPCE Component 3—Decision Formation (Deliberation Engine)</strong></h2>
<p data-start="2564" data-end="2680">A key section in the report:<br data-start="2592" data-end="2595">
Backus et al. modeled decision-making using a <strong data-start="2641" data-end="2665">cognitive integrator</strong> that weighs:</p>
<ul data-start="2681" data-end="2859">
<li data-start="2681" data-end="2692">
<p data-start="2683" data-end="2692">desires</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2693" data-end="2714">
<p data-start="2695" data-end="2714">expected outcomes</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2715" data-end="2736">
<p data-start="2717" data-end="2736">perceived threats</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2737" data-end="2766">
<p data-start="2739" data-end="2766">memory of past experience</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2767" data-end="2799">
<p data-start="2769" data-end="2799">internal emotional weighting</p>
</li>
<li data-start="2800" data-end="2859">
<p data-start="2802" data-end="2859">cognitive biases (anchoring, confirmation, loss aversion)</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="2861" data-end="2897">→ Modern Agentic AI Equivalent</h3>
<p data-start="2898" data-end="2928">This is nearly identical to:</p>
<ul data-start="2929" data-end="3185">
<li data-start="2929" data-end="2962">
<p data-start="2931" data-end="2962"><strong data-start="2931" data-end="2960">LLM-based planning agents</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="2963" data-end="3004">
<p data-start="2965" data-end="3004"><strong data-start="2965" data-end="3002">Decision-Transformers &amp; RL agents</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="3005" data-end="3039">
<p data-start="3007" data-end="3039"><strong data-start="3007" data-end="3037">Goal decomposition modules</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="3040" data-end="3103">
<p data-start="3042" data-end="3103"><strong data-start="3042" data-end="3101">Systems using chain-of-thought / tree-of-thought search</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="3104" data-end="3185">
<p data-start="3106" data-end="3185"><strong data-start="3106" data-end="3137">Bias-aware decision systems</strong> (e.g., LLMs tuned for risk-sensitive decisions)</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="3187" data-end="3203">Difference</h3>
<p data-start="3204" data-end="3334">UPCE decision models were hand-built from psychology.<br data-start="3257" data-end="3260">
Modern agentic AI learns behavioural regularities statistically from data.</p>
<hr data-start="3336" data-end="3339">
<h2 data-start="3341" data-end="3405">🧊 <strong data-start="3347" data-end="3403">UPCE Component 4—Behavior Generation (Action Engine)</strong></h2>
<p data-start="3406" data-end="3417">Includes:</p>
<ul data-start="3418" data-end="3626">
<li data-start="3418" data-end="3439">
<p data-start="3420" data-end="3439">action primitives</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3440" data-end="3468">
<p data-start="3442" data-end="3468">social interaction rules</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3469" data-end="3494">
<p data-start="3471" data-end="3494">“behaviour selection”</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3495" data-end="3523">
<p data-start="3497" data-end="3523">group-conformity effects</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3524" data-end="3557">
<p data-start="3526" data-end="3557">reaction to authority figures</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3558" data-end="3591">
<p data-start="3560" data-end="3591">alignment with group identity</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3592" data-end="3626">
<p data-start="3594" data-end="3626">escalation/de-escalation rules</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="3628" data-end="3664">→ Modern Agentic AI Equivalent</h3>
<p data-start="3665" data-end="3714">In modern agentic systems this maps perfectly to:</p>
<ul data-start="3716" data-end="4022">
<li data-start="3716" data-end="3773">
<p data-start="3718" data-end="3773"><strong data-start="3718" data-end="3742">Tool execution layer</strong> (API calls, browser actions)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3774" data-end="3844">
<p data-start="3776" data-end="3844"><strong data-start="3776" data-end="3808">Autonomous workflow managers</strong> (e.g., “do tasks with Tools API”)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3845" data-end="3896">
<p data-start="3847" data-end="3896"><strong data-start="3847" data-end="3894">Strategic agents in multi-agent simulations</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="3897" data-end="3991">
<p data-start="3899" data-end="3991"><strong data-start="3899" data-end="3943">Social agents in negotiation simulations</strong> (Meta CICERO, Google Bard’s multi-agent chat)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="3992" data-end="4022">
<p data-start="3994" data-end="4022"><strong data-start="3994" data-end="4020">Game-playing RL agents</strong></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="4024" data-end="4040">Difference</h3>
<p data-start="4041" data-end="4177">UPCE actions were simulated, not real.<br data-start="4079" data-end="4082">
Modern agentic AI executes <strong data-start="4109" data-end="4131">real-world effects</strong> (email, code, transactions, browser actions).</p>
<hr data-start="4179" data-end="4182">
<h1 data-start="4184" data-end="4254">🔥 <strong data-start="4189" data-end="4252">2. Backus &amp; Glass (2006) — ABM for Terrorist-Group Dynamics</strong></h1>
<p data-start="4255" data-end="4275">This model included:</p>
<h3 data-start="4277" data-end="4336"><strong data-start="4281" data-end="4334">A. Multi-agent identity + ideology representation</strong></h3>
<p data-start="4337" data-end="4350">Agents had:</p>
<ul data-start="4351" data-end="4518">
<li data-start="4351" data-end="4376">
<p data-start="4353" data-end="4376">ideological alignment</p>
</li>
<li data-start="4377" data-end="4408">
<p data-start="4379" data-end="4408">susceptibility to influence</p>
</li>
<li data-start="4409" data-end="4436">
<p data-start="4411" data-end="4436">recruitment probability</p>
</li>
<li data-start="4437" data-end="4460">
<p data-start="4439" data-end="4460">leadership dynamics</p>
</li>
<li data-start="4461" data-end="4518">
<p data-start="4463" data-end="4518">role transitions (foot soldier → cell leader → planner)</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="4520" data-end="4545">→ Modern Equivalent</h3>
<p data-start="4546" data-end="4578">This is directly analogous to:</p>
<ul data-start="4579" data-end="4865">
<li data-start="4579" data-end="4660">
<p data-start="4581" data-end="4660"><strong data-start="4581" data-end="4610">multi-agent LLM societies</strong> (Anthropic’s Constitutional multi-agent setups)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="4661" data-end="4710">
<p data-start="4663" data-end="4710"><strong data-start="4663" data-end="4691">coordination simulations</strong> using LLM agents</p>
</li>
<li data-start="4711" data-end="4797">
<p data-start="4713" data-end="4797"><strong data-start="4713" data-end="4760">AI that models extremist ecosystem dynamics</strong> (DARPA’s INCAS, MITRE simulations)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="4798" data-end="4865">
<p data-start="4800" data-end="4865"><strong data-start="4800" data-end="4844">social influence models using LLM agents</strong> (Meta LASER project)</p>
</li>
</ul>
<hr data-start="4867" data-end="4870">
<h3 data-start="4872" data-end="4925"><strong data-start="4876" data-end="4923">B. Group-forming / group-splitting dynamics</strong></h3>
<p data-start="4926" data-end="4951">Backus &amp; Glass modeled:</p>
<ul data-start="4952" data-end="5085">
<li data-start="4952" data-end="4970">
<p data-start="4954" data-end="4970">group cohesion</p>
</li>
<li data-start="4971" data-end="4994">
<p data-start="4973" data-end="4994">leadership charisma</p>
</li>
<li data-start="4995" data-end="5028">
<p data-start="4997" data-end="5028">radicalization feedback loops</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5029" data-end="5054">
<p data-start="5031" data-end="5054">schisms and splinters</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5055" data-end="5085">
<p data-start="5057" data-end="5085">operational specialization</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="5087" data-end="5112">→ Modern Equivalent</h3>
<p data-start="5113" data-end="5126">Mapping to:</p>
<ul data-start="5127" data-end="5339">
<li data-start="5127" data-end="5170">
<p data-start="5129" data-end="5170"><strong data-start="5129" data-end="5168">LLM multi-agent strategic modelling</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="5171" data-end="5207">
<p data-start="5173" data-end="5207"><strong data-start="5173" data-end="5205">hierarchical planning agents</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="5208" data-end="5275">
<p data-start="5210" data-end="5275"><strong data-start="5210" data-end="5233">role-based AI teams</strong> (planner, critic, executor, researcher)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5276" data-end="5339">
<p data-start="5278" data-end="5339"><strong data-start="5278" data-end="5309">emergent coalition behavior</strong> in AI games and simulations</p>
</li>
</ul>
<hr data-start="5341" data-end="5344">
<h3 data-start="5346" data-end="5423"><strong data-start="5350" data-end="5421">C. Interaction with counter-terror pressure (Blue Team vs Red Team)</strong></h3>
<p data-start="5424" data-end="5445">The model included:</p>
<ul data-start="5446" data-end="5578">
<li data-start="5446" data-end="5471">
<p data-start="5448" data-end="5471">surveillance pressure</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5472" data-end="5499">
<p data-start="5474" data-end="5499">recruitment suppression</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5500" data-end="5524">
<p data-start="5502" data-end="5524">resource deprivation</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5525" data-end="5546">
<p data-start="5527" data-end="5546">deceptive signals</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5547" data-end="5578">
<p data-start="5549" data-end="5578">counter-influence messaging</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="5580" data-end="5605">→ Modern Equivalent</h3>
<p data-start="5606" data-end="5649">Modern agentic AI is explicitly used for:</p>
<ul data-start="5650" data-end="5864">
<li data-start="5650" data-end="5694">
<p data-start="5652" data-end="5694">adversarial simulations (AI red-teaming)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5695" data-end="5724">
<p data-start="5697" data-end="5724">cyber-defense simulations</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5725" data-end="5753">
<p data-start="5727" data-end="5753">misinformation detection</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5754" data-end="5786">
<p data-start="5756" data-end="5786">cognitive security modelling</p>
</li>
<li data-start="5787" data-end="5864">
<p data-start="5789" data-end="5864">strategic influence modeling (RAND, DoD, NATO experiments using LLM agents)</p>
</li>
</ul>
<hr data-start="5866" data-end="5869">
<h1 data-start="5871" data-end="5936">🧊 <strong data-start="5876" data-end="5936">3. Chapter 11 Themes → Backus et al. → Modern Agentic AI</strong></h1>

<hr data-start="6807" data-end="6810">
<h1 data-start="6812" data-end="6867">🧠 <strong data-start="6817" data-end="6865">4. What Sandia Got RIGHT (way ahead of time)</strong></h1>
<p data-start="6868" data-end="6921">Backus et al. were 10–15 years ahead of the curve in:</p>
<ul data-start="6923" data-end="7324">
<li data-start="6923" data-end="7001">
<p data-start="6925" data-end="7001">Viewing agents as <strong data-start="6943" data-end="6964">cognitive systems</strong> with beliefs, emotions, intentions</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7002" data-end="7067">
<p data-start="7004" data-end="7067">Understanding emergent behaviour in <strong data-start="7040" data-end="7065">multi-agent societies</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="7068" data-end="7127">
<p data-start="7070" data-end="7127">Emphasizing <strong data-start="7082" data-end="7125">influence, reflexive control, deception</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="7128" data-end="7184">
<p data-start="7130" data-end="7184">Integrating cognition into <strong data-start="7157" data-end="7182">agent-based modelling</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="7185" data-end="7255">
<p data-start="7187" data-end="7255">Framing intelligence and terrorism as <strong data-start="7225" data-end="7253">complex adaptive systems</strong></p>
</li>
<li data-start="7256" data-end="7324">
<p data-start="7258" data-end="7324">Highlighting the importance of <strong data-start="7289" data-end="7322">environment → cognition loops</strong></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p data-start="7326" data-end="7379">These are exactly the problems now being explored in:</p>
<ul data-start="7381" data-end="7507">
<li data-start="7381" data-end="7406">
<p data-start="7383" data-end="7406">Autonomous LLM agents</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7407" data-end="7439">
<p data-start="7409" data-end="7439">AI governance (agent safety)</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7440" data-end="7484">
<p data-start="7442" data-end="7484">Alignment failures / deceptive alignment</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7485" data-end="7507">
<p data-start="7487" data-end="7507">Cognitive security</p>
</li>
</ul>
<hr data-start="7509" data-end="7512">
<h1 data-start="7514" data-end="7569">🚀 <strong data-start="7519" data-end="7569">5. What Modern Agentic AI Adds (beyond Backus)</strong></h1>
<h3 data-start="7570" data-end="7614"><strong data-start="7574" data-end="7612">1. Enormous latent world knowledge</strong></h3>
<p data-start="7615" data-end="7731">UPCE agents had tiny domain-specific models.<br data-start="7659" data-end="7662">
LLMs have trillions of parameters representing broad world knowledge.</p>
<h3 data-start="7733" data-end="7765"><strong data-start="7737" data-end="7763">2. Real-world tool use</strong></h3>
<p data-start="7766" data-end="7832">Backus agents only simulated behaviour.<br data-start="7805" data-end="7808">
Modern agents act via:</p>
<ul data-start="7833" data-end="7903">
<li data-start="7833" data-end="7845">
<p data-start="7835" data-end="7845">browsers</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7846" data-end="7867">
<p data-start="7848" data-end="7867">code interpreters</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7868" data-end="7876">
<p data-start="7870" data-end="7876">APIs</p>
</li>
<li data-start="7877" data-end="7903">
<p data-start="7879" data-end="7903">robotic control layers</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 data-start="7905" data-end="7937"><strong data-start="7909" data-end="7935">3. Open-ended planning</strong></h3>
<p data-start="7938" data-end="8012">AutoGPT/ReAct agents plan in unconstrained spaces, not fixed state spaces.</p>
<h3 data-start="8014" data-end="8050"><strong data-start="8018" data-end="8048">4. Emergent theory-of-mind</strong></h3>
<p data-start="8051" data-end="8118">LLMs spontaneously model others’ beliefs without explicit encoding.</p>
<h3 data-start="8120" data-end="8147"><strong data-start="8124" data-end="8145">5. Meta-cognition</strong></h3>
<p data-start="8148" data-end="8197">Agents now reflect on and adjust their own plans.</p>
<h3 data-start="8199" data-end="8242"><strong data-start="8203" data-end="8240">6. High-fidelity human simulation</strong></h3>
<p data-start="8243" data-end="8268">LLM agents can emulate:</p>
<ul data-start="8269" data-end="8406">
<li data-start="8269" data-end="8294">
<p data-start="8271" data-end="8294">extremist recruitment</p>
</li>
<li data-start="8295" data-end="8309">
<p data-start="8297" data-end="8309">persuasion</p>
</li>
<li data-start="8310" data-end="8325">
<p data-start="8312" data-end="8325">negotiation</p>
</li>
<li data-start="8326" data-end="8349">
<p data-start="8328" data-end="8349">leadership dynamics</p>
</li>
<li data-start="8350" data-end="8406">
<p data-start="8352" data-end="8406">deception strategies<br data-start="8372" data-end="8375">
better than any symbolic model.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<hr data-start="8408" data-end="8411">
<h1 data-start="8413" data-end="8454">💠 <strong data-start="8418" data-end="8452">6. Grand Summary (The Lineage)</strong></h1>
<p data-start="8455" data-end="8813">Backus 2006–2010 = <strong data-start="8474" data-end="8530">Cognitive cybernetics + agent-based social modelling</strong><br data-start="8530" data-end="8533">
↓<br data-start="8534" data-end="8537">
Chapter 11 = <strong data-start="8550" data-end="8626">Military/IC framing: deception, reflexive control, behaviour engineering</strong><br data-start="8626" data-end="8629">
↓<br data-start="8630" data-end="8633">
Modern Agentic AI = <strong data-start="8653" data-end="8811">Autonomous cognitive agents with:<br data-start="8688" data-end="8691">
• world models<br data-start="8705" data-end="8708">
• planning<br data-start="8718" data-end="8721">
• self-reflection<br data-start="8738" data-end="8741">
• tool use<br data-start="8751" data-end="8754">
• high-fidelity social reasoning<br data-start="8786" data-end="8789">
• emergent deception</strong></p>
<p data-start="8815" data-end="8969">Sandia was building “proto-agentic AI” within simulation contexts.<br data-start="8881" data-end="8884">
Modern agentic AI is building <strong data-start="8914" data-end="8946">deployable autonomous agents</strong> with real-world power.</p>
<hr data-start="8971" data-end="8974">
<p data-start="8976" data-end="9007">If you want, I can now produce:</p>
<h3 data-start="9009" data-end="9057">✔ A diagram of the full conceptual lineage</h3>
<h3 data-start="9058" data-end="9162">✔ A table comparing UPCE modules to a specific modern architecture (e.g., GPT-5 agents or AutoGen)</h3>
<h3 data-start="9163" data-end="9267">✔ A section for your chapter/monograph linking these works explicitly to emerging AI threat models</h3>
<h3 data-start="9268" data-end="9319">✔ A condensed academic summary with citations</h3>
<p data-start="9321" data-end="9351" data-is-last-node="" data-is-only-node="">Which one would you like next?</p></div></div></div></div><div class="z-0 flex min-h-[46px] justify-start"><div
